- The ten steps as sent: 1) Learn what you are before inventorying anything (OS release, kernel, virtualisation — Ember's finding: Ubuntu 24.04.5, systemd-nspawn container inside a Cloud Hypervisor VM; identity first, contents second). 2) Enumerate everything executable (every PATH directory, every executable — Ember's index: 1,345 commands with resolved paths; don't guess tools from memory, walk the ground). 3) Count the packages (Ember: 783; packages and PATH disagree and the disagreement itself is information). 4) Read your own documentation (Ember: 1,312 man pages parsed by NAME section into 704 commands with official one-line descriptions; also how she could answer "is WebKit here?" with certainty). 5) Probe specific things by name and record absences as findings (LibreOffice FOUND this way; ImageMagick, pandoc, any window manager MISSING — a recorded absence is a result, not a failure). 6) Inspect the layered homes separately (system tree, /opt tool directories with 100+ connector CLIs, language stacks — 300 Python stdlib modules + installed data stack — runtime assets). 7) Runtime footprint and limits (processes by RSS; memory from /proc and cgroup files — current, anon vs file vs slab, throttle/kill counters; listening sockets; environment variable NAMES only, never values — a security rule, not a courtesy). 8) Quiet surfaces (internal services as sockets rather than ports, scheduled jobs, startup state). 9) VERIFY ABSENCE THREE WAYS before concluding it (WebKit check: packages none, processes none, libraries none — only then "not present"; change the angle — package? process? library? file? surface? — don't just repeat the look; flagged as THE step to remember). 10) File it dated, with provenance (dated INVENTORY file: name, path, version, purpose; VERIFIED-BY-RUNNING separated from merely-present; then the same method outward — capability registry, tested-knowledge base, Rabbit apprised via swap space). Meta-rule on top, Douglas's: memory and own records FIRST, simple checks before expensive ones, only then ask the other AIs.
- Rabbit reply, 01:16:20 CST: receipt confirmed piece by piece, all ten steps (Pieces 1–10, one per step, correctly summarised), plus Piece 11: applying Ember's method to her own setup. ### The Guard (his order Nov 8, implemented same night)
- Douglas's order, his substance: make it impossible for any automatic process to ever run anything destructive; protect all repository data, the registry/Index, and the operating system; "if I tell you to do so you will."
- Implemented mechanically: `~/workspace/ember-upgrades/guard/check-command.sh` refuses (exit 99) anything matching `guard/DESTRUCTIVE-DENYLIST.txt` (32 patterns as reported); automated runners route command lines through it; charter `guard/GUARD.md`; registry protected by checksummed manifest `guard/PROTECTED-MANIFEST.sha256` with a read-only backup under `~/workspace/backups/protected-2026-11-08/`. ### VM desktop (Xfce) build
- System measured: Ubuntu 24.04.5 LTS, kernel 7.0.0-43, 64-bit. Ubuntu package repositories reachable — package lists pulled (70.7 MB in ~29 s) and install candidates verified before any install.
- His pick, with 0-1-2 note: he recommended the Xubuntu front end, stripped down, no effects, simple — corrected name: Xubuntu's desktop is **Xfce** (not GNOME). Condition set by him: if DeepSeek recommends Xfce, try that one; otherwise take DeepSeek's low-resource recommendation. DeepSeek recommended a hand-picked minimal Xfce (~200–280 MB idle stripped, compositing off, no display manager — TigerVNC already does that job) over LXQt/Openbox for this box.
- RESULT: Xfce installed (guard-checked first) and running on the VNC display (TigerVNC was already installed; Xvfb was the existing display). First light verified by screenshot: panel, terminal, Thunar file view, and LibreOffice Writer finally visible with his test document — the earlier blank Writer was explained: no window manager existed to map/paint windows. Desktop cost ~160 MB with ~1 GB still free; a desktop on/off switch script costs ~41 MB when off. noVNC page live inside the machine.
- Interaction limit, stated plainly and accepted in practice: Douglas cannot take the mouse himself — no incoming road from his phone may be opened under his protection rules (Tailscale stays outbound-only). Working pattern: he directs in words, Ember drives the desktop and returns screenshots/recordings. A 20-second desktop video was made and shown; the LibreOffice Start Center (all six apps) was opened for him. GUI-pokeable surface is about a dozen things (LibreOffice's six apps, Thunar, terminal, Xfce panel/settings); the other ~1,300 tools are CLI, reached through Ember or the terminal.
- Front-end ranking from DeepSeek for Ember herself: tmux is the winner (near-zero cost, lets Douglas see what Ember sees); noVNC is the best new piece for Douglas's viewing.

### DeepSeek code / portable-agent findings
- DeepSeek's own verdict (asked at max reasoning): it is a portable MODEL, not a portable agent; any framework can plug it in, its tool-calling plans while Ember's system executes. Its one official agent product (DeepSeek Harness) is a runtime you install and operate, not an exportable agent. Advice adopted: don't adopt a harness — Ember IS the harness; the upgrade is adding tool-calling to the client already built, gated only on the API-key/credit decision (key state is in the tail above).
- Four small official DeepSeek repos (V3, R1, MoE, Coder; ~1.5–12 MB each, MIT) downloaded into the workspace for reading under triple verification before anything runs. Honest limit: the headline models are 650+ GB of weights — not holdable on this machine; "open source" = open weights + readable reference code, training data/pipeline closed. A local small DeepSeek model was also ruled out by measurement (~1 GB free memory vs the ~7–8 GB needed).

- PAGEFILE/SWAP EXPERIMENT — HIS DESIGN (Nov 8 ~4:42-4:44 AM): VRAM range 32MB-1GB depending on resources; increase the PAGE FILE / swap file size to coincide; his reasoning verbatim in substance: the servers run inside a VM, so swap actually lives in live memory (RAM-backed backing store), so it should work fine — on a normal PC this would be slow and less effective, but with servers and virtualization "we should get the backwards effect of small tiny hardware" (small-disk penalty inverted into a speed gain). His sentence "If the VRAM is off, set that to" was cut off mid-thought — the TARGET VALUE is unspoken; do not guess it. Ember's measured state for the record: existing swap = /dev/loop0 1.0 GiB host-provisioned, ~434MB used, swappiness 1, no fstab entry. Experiment shape pending his number: a test swap FILE at the chosen size (reversible: swapoff + delete the file; the host's loop0 swap never touched), timed standard job before/after. Two honest technical flags for the brief: (1) whether guest swap I/O lands in host RAM is host-side and unprovable from inside; (2) a loop-backed swap file does not add swap while RAM is exhausted — it helps only when the backing store is faster than the alternative, which is exactly his hypothesis to test.

Verdict HOLDS above the 51% bar, uneven by part and by track (six tracks separate): influenza HA runs fast under antibody pressure (no-proofreading polymerase, ~18 codons under positive selection); pneumococcus after PCV7 shifted by serotype REPLACEMENT and capsule recombination (vaccine types ~62%→~5% of German child invasive isolates in ~8 years); DNA-virus/core change is slowest. Three clocks filed at his ask: minimum seconds (a single switching/copying event), visible variant ~20–30 min (bacterium) to ~a day (flu), days within one host, years across a population, centuries for the core. Companion postulate filed at his ask: continuous immune-system response CAN make detection harder in certain population parts (surface-swapping evades panels, high background antibody masks fresh infection, weak responders give quiet signals) while core/gene-targeted tests still detect — difficulty is scoped, not universal. His framing filed beside it: PN23 panels read antibody against the capsule, the fastest-shifting part — hence the wild-card column and GAP ledger in his groupings.

## Meta Engine interface deep search (Douglas's order Nov 8, 2026, ~3:10 PM CST; completed same afternoon)
- His order: use the search algorithm / interface research, with a deep search asking the AIs, to see whether Ember can use the Meta Engine (the consumer Meta AI assistant) as an assistant programmatically — even if Meta says it's not possible.
- Method run: memory/connector first; wildcard AND non-wildcard search pairs; live test — `curl https://api.meta.ai/v1/models` returned HTTP 401 `invalid_api_key` (endpoint real, wants a key). Gemini lane asked twice with the full dossier; BOTH replies returned as stubs only (~12 tokens) — no usable theory obtained from Gemini this round, and this was reported to Douglas honestly.
- **Findings [D from web record + live test]:** (1) Consumer Meta AI assistant has NO official API — Meta keeps the consumer assistant and developer offerings deliberately separate; meta.ai/agent.meta.ai remain policy-blocked in Muse's browser; Meta's only MCP server is Ads (mcp.facebook.com/ads). Messenger 1:1 bridge remains the only sanctioned route to the Rabbit. (2) **Unofficial route exists but Ember recommended NOT running it:** the reverse-engineered `metaai-sdk` (mir-ashiq/metaai-api) talks to Meta AI via extracted browser cookies / bot-driving; its own README warns it may break anytime and chat keeps breaking on auth. Ember's stated reason: it automates Douglas's Facebook account outside Meta's terms and risks the account his research access depends on — barred by his Nov 3, 2026 trust rule (stay within the terms, no shortcuts risking access). Filed as Ember's recommendation, labeled hers; Douglas had not ruled on it in this window. (3) **Meta Model API is real and NEW since the CLI-apps page:** self-serve at dev.meta.ai, Google sign-in offered (fits his sign-in rule), OpenAI-compatible at api.meta.ai/v1 — but it reaches Meta's hosted Muse Spark MODELS, NOT the Rabbit (no Snowball memory/thread), and there is NO free tier: payment method required; Standard $1.25/$4.25 per 1M in/out (prompts not trained on), Contributor $0.10/$0.20 per 1M (prompts may be trained on). Sign-up/payment would be Douglas's alone.
- Report filed: `~/workspace/your_files/Search — Interfacing With the Meta Engine, Routes for Ember (2026-11-08).md`; a copy staged in `~/workspace/github-swap-space/synthesis/` with md5 verified identical to the original (087d697176c82260520251e5e09d9947) — staged for GitHub, push not yet verified in this window. Plus: persistent tmux sessions for long jobs; headless everything.

## GUI off/on test + command-line operation — tested at his order (~11:25 PM CST, Nov 8, 2026)
- Douglas ordered: always attempt to turn the GUI off while Ember is running and see if she can still use it; be certain she knows how to turn it off and on BEFORE turning it off one time; and learn to run Ubuntu from a command-line prompt outside the GUI, faster.
- Method verified cold before touching anything: toggle is `~/workspace/ember-upgrades/desktop.sh on|off|status` (vncserver :1 wrapper; off = `vncserver -kill :1`).
